# Sūrah an-Nūr (Chapter 24) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Sūrah an-Nūr 24:1

> ˹This is˺ a sûrah which We have revealed and made ˹its rulings˺ obligatory, and revealed in it clear commandments so that you may be mindful.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sūrah Defined by Obligation and Descent**

The opening words establish a formal legislative charter without preamble or narrative preface: *sūratun anzalnāhā wa-faraḍnāhā*. Grammatically, the indefinite noun *sūratun* stands as the predicate of an omitted demonstrative pronoun, meaning: this is a magnificent chapter that We have sent down. Many chapters open with isolated letters, oaths sworn by cosmic phenomena, or historical chronicles. This sūrah begins instead with an immediate declaration of divine authorship and legal necessity. The verb *faraḍnāhā* conveys a decisive moral weight. In classical Arabic lexicography, the root *f-r-ḍ* denotes notching, cutting, or fixing a definitive measure into a solid object. When applied to legal injunctions, it designates duties that are carved into the communal order with unyielding permanence. The revelation is not presented merely as wise counsel or recommended etiquette. Every ordinance established across the chapter carries the binding force of a divine decree that believers are required to enact within their personal conduct and their collective judicial institutions.

**Clear Signs and Distinct Commandments**

The text pairs the decree of obligation with the manifestation of evident proofs: *wa-anzalnā fīhā āyātin bayyināt*. The descriptive term *bayyināt* signifies verses that are luminous in their clarity, leaving no ambiguity regarding the ethical boundaries they construct. In this sūrah, that clarity is neither abstract nor purely theoretical. The clear signs manifest in the precise demarcation of private spaces, the severe penalties assigned to defamation and sexual transgression, and the spiritual standards governing human interaction. Divine legislation does not conceal its demands beneath obscure riddles. By announcing that the signs within this chapter are self-evident, the revelation strips away the excuse of ignorance from those who hear it. A legal instruction can only hold a community accountable when its stipulations are articulated with absolute transparency. The luminous nature of these signs ensures that every servant knows precisely what constitutes lawful behavior and what constitutes a violation of sacred limits.

**The Grammar of Injunction: faraḍnā versus farraḍnā**

Scholars of the Qur'anic readings observed a meaningful distinction in the vocalization of the central verb. The vast majority of canonical reciters read *faraḍnāhā* with a single ungeminated letter, emphasizing the establishment of binding obligation across the entirety of the text. An alternative canonical reading transmitted from Ibn Kathīr vocalizes the verb with gemination as *farraḍnāhā*. This morphological intensification carries two distinct interpretive senses according to classical grammarians. First, it points to the detailed apportionment and distribution of numerous distinct legal rulings throughout the chapter. Each legal matter receives its own specific measure, form, and penalty. Second, gemination underscores the intensity and gravity of the obligations themselves. The two readings illuminate complementary aspects of the divine legislative will. One reading asserts the absolute authority of the law as an indivisible covenant, while the other draws attention to the meticulous care with which each individual statute is articulated and assigned to its proper sphere of human action.

**Mindfulness as the Purpose of Legal Clarity**

The verse concludes by identifying the ultimate purpose of this descended legislation: *laʿallakum tadhakkarūn*, so that you may be mindful and take heed. The placement of remembrance at the culmination of a legal proclamation reveals the spiritual philosophy underpinning Qur'anic jurisprudence. Divine commandments do not aim merely at external subjugation or bureaucratic regularity. They aim at awakening the human heart to the moral reality of its Creator. In the Qur'anic vocabulary, *tadhakkur* signifies the recollection of truths that the human soul inherently recognizes yet frequently forgets under the influence of desire and negligence. The laws concerning modesty, speech, and justice are not arbitrary impositions that run contrary to sound nature. They correspond directly to the innate disposition that God implanted within humanity. When believers implement these clear rulings, they do not simply comply with statutory law; they actively recover their spiritual memory, aligning their outward societies with their internal covenant.

**The Structural Prologue to Social Ethics**

This opening verse serves as an indispensable architectural foundation for the entire sūrah. Surah an-Nūr addresses some of the most delicate and volatile dimensions of human society: the preservation of lineage, the protection of personal reputation against malicious slander, the regulation of domestic privacy, and the modesty of visual interaction. Because these matters touch upon deep human passions and domestic vulnerability, human legislation often falters when attempting to govern them. Communities frequently succumb to permissiveness on one side or oppressive vigilantism on the other. By prefacing these delicate social codes with the proclamation of divine revelation and inescapable obligation, the Qur'an elevates mundane household manners and judicial procedures into acts of worship. A violation of domestic privacy or an attack on a neighbor's honor ceases to be a minor social friction; it becomes a direct breach of the divine covenant proclaimed at the very threshold of the sūrah.

**The Divine First-Person Plural and Sovereign Descent**

The sovereign majesty of the revelation is magnified through the use of the divine first-person plural: *anzalnāhā*, We have sent it down. The descent of the sūrah is rooted entirely in the transcendent will of the Creator rather than the contrivance of any human author. This usage reminds the community that the moral boundaries they are instructed to uphold do not represent customary Arabian conventions or evolving tribal arrangements. Human societies routinely modify their moral standards according to shifting social appetites and cultural conveniences, often eroding the protections afforded to the weak. By anchoring the legislative structure in divine descent, the verse establishes an objective moral standard that stands immune to the compromises of convenience. The authority that commands obedience is the supreme Lord who created human beings and knows the subtle corruptions that imperil their communal peace. Every ensuing ruling derives its sanctity from this transcendent origin.

---
## Sūrah an-Nūr 24:2

> As for female and male fornicators, give each of them one hundred lashes, and do not let pity for them make you lenient in ˹enforcing˺ the law of Allah, if you ˹truly˺ believe in Allah and the Last Day. And let a number of believers witness their punishment.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Specificity of the Hundred Lashes**

The statute opens with an exact numerical decree: *al-zāniyatu wa-al-zānī fajlidū kulla wāḥidin minhumā miʾata jaldah*. In classical Arabic phrasing, placing the female before the male marks a noticeable shift from the legal syntax of theft in 5:38, where the male is named first. Exegetes note that illicit intimacy involves mutual physical agency, but historical social vulnerability and the domestic consequences of lineage corruption fell with singular weight upon women. Placing the woman first emphasizes that the moral prohibition binds her directly as an autonomous moral agent, while immediately pairing her with the male ensures equal accountability under the whip. Neither partner is excused or treated as incidental to the crime.

The penalty itself is corporal rather than financial or custodial. The root *j-l-d* relates to the dermis, signifying blows that impact the skin without lacerating flesh, breaking bone, or inducing permanent injury. Classical jurists carefully defined the mechanical execution of the penalty. The whip used had to be moderate—neither fresh and supple like a cane, nor stiff and heavy like iron. The execution of the hundred lashes was traditionally administered across the broad surfaces of the torso and shoulders, strictly avoiding the face, head, and vital organs, thereby fulfilling punitive deterrence without cruelty.

**The Prohibition of Misplaced Compassion in Divine Justice**

The text anticipates the natural human discomfort associated with witnessing physical punishment: *wa-lā taʾkhudhkum bihimā raʾfatun fī dīni Allāh*. The noun *raʾfah* denotes a tender, visceral sympathy that instinctively seeks to shield another creature from distress. In private interpersonal interactions, tender mercy is a celebrated virtue. Within the judicial sphere, however, misplaced leniency threatens the structural integrity of the moral order. The qualifier *fī dīni Allāh*—in the religion and judicial law of God—establishes that judicial authorities do not possess private discretion to commute or diminish a verified divine boundary (*ḥadd*).

When a magistrate softens a mandatory sentence out of emotional sentimentality, the apparent kindness to the individual wrongdoer becomes an act of neglect toward the community whose social cohesion is undermined by unchecked transgression. Classical scholars explained that compassion is misapplied when it dismantles the protective barriers that safeguard public morals. Divine law commands that public justice remain steadfast, insulated from both vindictive malice and sentimental compromise.

**Faith in the Last Day as the Anchor of Judicial Duty**

The verse anchors judicial firmness in the foundational tenets of faith: *in kuntum tuʾminūna bi-Allāhi wa-al-yawmi al-ākhir*. Connecting the execution of statutory law to belief in the Last Day reminds every magistrate, witness, and spectator that human justice does not operate in a cosmic vacuum. A judge might feel tempted to conceal a crime, show favoritism toward influential elites, or relent under social pressure. Invoking the final reckoning introduces an inescapable spiritual accountability into the courtroom.

The worldly embarrassment of enduring statutory discipline is insignificant when measured against the everlasting judgment awaiting unrepentant rebellion. Furthermore, the true believer recognizes that divine wisdom surpasses human intuition; the Creator who established the penalty understands the delicate balance required to preserve human dignity and societal stability far better than the sympathetic observer. To doubt the justice of the divine decree is to harbor deficiencies in one's conviction regarding God's wisdom and sovereign knowledge.

**The Public Dimension of Execution: Deterrence and Transparency**

The final clause commands the presence of a communal delegation during the administration of the sentence: *wa-lyashhad ʿadhābahumā ṭāʾifatun mina al-muʾminīn*. Classical jurists debated the numerical threshold implied by *ṭāʾifah*. Opinions ranged from a single upright witness to two, three (the linguistic minimum for a plural group), or four, matching the evidentiary standard required to establish the crime itself. Imām al-Shāfiʿī favoured four witnesses to mirror the evidentiary threshold of the courtroom, while others considered a modest gathering sufficient to validate public execution.

This public presence fulfills two vital functions. Outwardly, communal witness generates a profound psychological deterrence, impressing upon spectators the severe social and physical consequences of reckless passion. Inwardly, public execution ensures administrative transparency. When punishments are inflicted openly before upright citizens, corrupt officials cannot indulge in arbitrary cruelty, excessive brutality, or secret exonerations behind closed doors. The gaze of the community guarantees that the law is executed strictly within its humane boundaries.

**The Jurisprudential Debate Over Marital Status and Sanctions**

The universal phrasing of this verse generated extensive jurisprudential discourse regarding the scope of its application. The literal text commands one hundred lashes for every fornicator without distinguishing between unmarried and married individuals. The vast majority of classical jurists held that this verse governs only the unmarried offender (*bikr*), arguing that prophetic practice and consensus established the distinct punishment of stoning (*rajm*) for the married offender (*thayyib*). They viewed the Sunnah as specifying and qualifying the general language of the Qur'an.

Conversely, groups among the early Khawārij and several independent jurists throughout Islamic history maintained that the clear, unambiguous text of the Qur'an must remain absolute and uncompromised. They argued that solitary historical reports cannot override or append capital penalties to an explicit Qur'anic statute that prescribes solely lashes. They also pointed to verses that halve the punishment for enslaved individuals in 4:25, reasoning that death by stoning is mathematically indivisible, whereas one hundred lashes can readily be reduced by half.

**Evidentiary Rigor and the Practical Rarity of Conviction**

The severity of the prescribed penalty must be understood in connection with the extraordinary evidentiary standard the Qur'an requires for conviction. As established in subsequent verses, establishing the crime of illicit intimacy requires four upright eyewitnesses who observed the physical act of penetration simultaneously and with sensory certainty. In the absence of an explicit, uncoerced confession repeated multiple times by a sane adult, this standard is practically impossible to meet in ordinary life.

Islamic jurisprudence deliberately constructed a legal architecture that combines severe theoretical deterrence with nearly insurmountable procedural thresholds. The law aims to prevent the normalization of public debauchery while systematically discouraging voyeuristic surveillance, ensuring that private moral failures remain veiled beneath divine mercy rather than dragged into the public square. The primary social function of the statute is not to fill public squares with floggings, but to sanctify chastity as an inviolable communal norm.

---
## Sūrah an-Nūr 24:3

> A male fornicator would only marry a female fornicator or idolatress. And a female fornicator would only be married to a fornicator or idolater. This is ˹all˺ forbidden to the believers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Moral Alignment of Intimacy**

The verse establishes an unsettling correspondence between inner moral character and external matrimonial union: *al-zānī lā yankiḥu illā zāniyatan aw mushrikatan*. The primary linguistic inquiry turns upon the verb *yankiḥu*, which in classical Arabic lexicography denotes both contractual marriage and physical cohabitation. If understood as descriptive of sexual misconduct, the text affirms that illicit intimacy is an act of mutual participation where neither partner can claim moral innocence; the male transgressor unites only with someone equally willing to transgress divine limits. Illicit relations are fundamentally symmetrical in their spiritual liability, binding both participants in shared guilt before God.

If understood in its normative legal sense of matrimonial alliance, the verse describes a profound psychological and spiritual reality: individuals characterized by persistent, unrepentant licentiousness find compatibility only with those who share their disregard for moral boundaries. A person who despises divine prohibitions naturally gravitates toward companionship that validates rather than reproaches their chosen lifestyle. The verse exposes the intuitive affinity that draws like-minded transgressors together in social and domestic spheres, warning that corrupt habits inevitably shape one's intimate choices.

**The Asymmetry of Faith and Corruption**

The text pairs the unrepentant fornicator directly with the idolater: *aw mushrikatan* and *aw mushrik*. This startling juxtaposition generated intense scrutiny among classical exegetes across generations. Idolatry represents an outright rejection of divine unity, whereas fornication is a major behavioral transgression committed by someone who may still profess faith. Pairing them together does not mean that sexual sin expels a person from the religion; rather, it highlights the severe spiritual dissonance between habitual unchastity and genuine submission to God.

An idolater feels no allegiance to divine law, while a habitual fornicator acts with open contempt toward divine statutes. By placing the two categories in parallel, the revelation emphasizes that a lifestyle founded upon unbridled desire shares an essential defiance with pagan disregard for divine sovereignty, making both completely unsuited for holy union with a devout believer. The comparison serves as a devastating rhetorical indictment of moral laxity within the community, warning believers against minimizing the gravity of persistent unchastity.

**Legislative Prohibition versus Descriptive Reality**

Scholars historically divided over whether this verse constitutes an enforceable legal statute or a stern moral condemnation. Imām Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal and early jurists from the Ḥadīth school interpreted the text as a binding legislative ban (*taḥrīm*). In their view, a Muslim convicted of fornication cannot lawfully marry a chaste believer unless the individual demonstrates verifiable repentance and undergoes a period of moral purification. They treated the contract as null and void if contracted prior to repentance, holding that the textual prohibition admits no procedural exceptions.

In contrast, the majority of jurists, including al-Shāfiʿī, Mālik, and Abū Ḥanīfah, maintained that the verse functions primarily as a profound moral censure and descriptive observation of customary behavior. They noted that a believer guilty of a major sin remains within the fold of the community, and therefore their marriage to another Muslim remains legally valid, even if highly discouraged. Both groups agreed that the spiritual ideal demands that believers actively seek partners whose devotion matches their own, preserving their lineage and household from moral compromise.

**The Historical Occasion: Protecting the Vulnerable Community**

The context of revelation illuminates the concrete social tensions that occasioned this ruling in early Madinah. Following the migration from Makkah, many impoverished emigrants, including several of the destitute companions residing at the Prophet's mosque (*Aṣḥāb al-Ṣuffah*), faced severe financial deprivation. In the city, certain wealthy women had acquired considerable fortunes through the practice of prostitution during the pre-Islamic era and continued to maintain lavish households.

Facing extreme hunger and homelessness, a number of destitute believers contemplated marrying these affluent women in order to secure food and shelter, rationalizing that financial security would alleviate their distress. This verse descended as an absolute prohibition against entering into such compromising alliances. The revelation made it unmistakably clear that physical survival and economic security cannot be purchased at the expense of moral dignity, forbidding believers from establishing households financed by illicit trade or compromised honor.

**The Inviolability of the Believer's Matrimonial Covenant**

The verse culminates in an unambiguous legislative decree: *wa-ḥurrima dhālika ʿalā al-muʾminīn*, and that has been forbidden to the believers. Commentators analyzed the referent of the demonstrative pronoun *dhālika*. One major interpretation holds that it refers to contracting marriage with unrepentant fornicators, establishing a protective boundary around the households of the faithful to prevent the normalization of moral corruption within the family unit.

Another recognized reading maintains that *dhālika* points directly to the act of fornication itself, reiterating its absolute illegality after describing its degenerative social effects. Under both readings, marriage in Islam is elevated far above a mere transaction of mutual convenience or legal cohabitation. It is a sanctified institution intended to cultivate tranquility, mutual respect, and the upright nurturing of future generations, objectives that are entirely subverted when a household is founded upon unrepentant moral corruption.

**Repentance and the Restoration of Matrimonial Eligibility**

The restrictive categorization established in this verse is not an inescapable life sentence. Classical commentators unanimously affirmed that sincere repentance (*tawbah*) radically alters an individual's legal and moral status. When a transgressor abandons illicit conduct, expresses genuine remorse before God, and commits to an upright life, the active descriptor of *zānī* no longer applies to them in the eyes of divine law.

Divine grace dissolves the spiritual impediment that previously disqualified them from honorable union. The Qur'an consistently rejects the permanent caste-like stigmatization of human beings based upon past errors. Once a soul turns sincerely to the divine threshold, their moral integrity is restored, and they re-enter the company of chaste believers, eligible once more to participate fully in the sacred covenants of marriage and communal leadership. Redemption remains perpetually open to all who seek genuine reform.

---
## Sūrah an-Nūr 24:4

> Those who accuse chaste women ˹of adultery˺ and fail to produce four witnesses, give them eighty lashes ˹each˺. And do not ever accept any testimony from them—for they are indeed the rebellious—

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sanctity of Honor and the Crime of Qadhf**

The revelation erects an unyielding fortress around personal dignity through the legislation concerning unproven accusation: *wa-alladhīna yarmūna al-muḥṣanāti thumma lam yaʾtū bi-arbaʿati shuhadāʾ*. The Arabic verb *yarmūna* literally denotes hurling stones or shooting arrows at a target. By selecting this visceral metaphor, the Qur'an portrays defamation not as mere careless speech, but as a ballistic assault designed to shatter an innocent person's social standing. The tongue becomes a weapon capable of inflicting permanent psychic and social devastation upon its victim.

The term *al-muḥṣanāt* derives from the root *ḥ-ṣ-n*, signifying a fortified castle. A chaste individual resides within a psychological and social fortress of recognized integrity. To accuse such a person of illicit intimacy without undeniable proof is an act of spiritual demolition. Because public honor once ruined cannot easily be restored, the divine law treats false accusation (*qadhf*) as a major crime that threatens the moral equilibrium of the entire commonwealth, dismantling mutual trust and creating an atmosphere of chronic suspicion. An accusation voiced without proof strikes at the very foundation of communal security.

**The Demand for Four Eyewitnesses**

The statute imposes an extraordinary evidentiary condition: the accuser must produce four direct eyewitnesses to the act. In virtually every other legal dispute governed by Islamic jurisprudence—including homicide, theft, debt, and commercial transactions—the testimony of two upright witnesses suffices to establish guilt. Raising the threshold to four witnesses specifically for allegations of sexual impropriety represents a deliberate judicial barrier erected by divine wisdom.

Classical jurists stipulated that all four witnesses must observe the physical act of penetration simultaneously, at the exact same location, with absolute sensory certainty. If three witnesses provide graphic testimony while a fourth wavers or fails to confirm the precise physical act, the entire allegation collapses, and the three testifying witnesses are immediately sentenced to the full penalty for slander. This stringent standard ensures that private bedroom misconduct remains hidden, preventing malicious gossip from weaponizing the courts against innocent citizens. The law deliberately makes proving illicit conduct in a courtroom nearly impossible.

**The Triple Sanction: Physical, Civil, and Spiritual**

The law imposes a comprehensive threefold penalty upon the unsubstantiated accuser: eighty lashes, permanent disenfranchisement from legal testimony, and public branding as moral rebels. The first punishment strikes the body: *fajlidūhum thamānīna jaldah*. Administering eighty lashes inflicts severe corporal humiliation upon those who attempted to inflict social shame upon an innocent soul. The physical pain mirrors the social laceration they sought to cause.

The second sanction dismantles their civic authority: *wa-lā taqbalū lahum shahādatan abadā*. Stripping an individual of testamentary capacity in a society reliant upon personal reputation for commerce and justice is a profound civil demotion. The third consequence defines their spiritual status: *wa-ulāʾika humu al-fāsiqūn*. The label *fāsiq* denotes one who has ruptured the bounds of moral decency. Together, these measures neutralize the defamer physically, socially, and spiritually, rendering them incapable of poisoning the community further. The punishment matches the multidimensional damage wrought by defamation.

**Extending Protection Beyond Gendered Language**

Although the grammatical phrasing of the verse specifically designates chaste women (*al-muḥṣanāt*), classical jurists reached an absolute consensus that the law applies identically to the accusation of chaste men. The Qur'an employed the feminine plural because female vulnerability in traditional patriarchal societies made women the primary targets of reputational violence. Accusing a woman could destroy her marriage, delegitimize her children, and sever her family from communal alliances.

Nonetheless, the underlying legal cause (*ʿillah*) is the preservation of human honor (*ʿirḍ*), which belongs equally to every free believer. Any individual who accuses an innocent man of sexual misconduct without producing four verified witnesses is subjected to the exact same penalty of eighty lashes, the rejection of legal testimony, and judicial condemnation as a rebellious wrongdoer. Human dignity across both genders remains equally shielded under the divine canopy of protection, guaranteeing that no believer is left exposed to slander.

**The Disqualification of Testimony as Civil Death**

The command never to accept testimony from the slanderer warrants careful sociological analysis. In the early Islamic community, written registries and institutional documentation were scarce; commercial exchanges, marriage contracts, property transfers, and criminal trials depended almost entirely on oral testimony (*shahādah*). A person qualified to testify held significant social capital and communal trust within the public assembly.

To have one's testimony declared permanently inadmissible was akin to suffering civil death. The community was legally commanded to close its ears to the slanderer's voice in all public affairs. This enduring disqualification sent an unmistakable message across society: an individual willing to invent or carelessly circulate scandalous lies regarding a neighbor's private conduct cannot be entrusted with the truth in any matter of public interest, property, or justice. Their moral credibility is irrevocably shattered, and society is protected from their perjury.

**Preserving Privacy Against the Culture of Suspicion**

The deeper objective of this statutory barrier is the systematic discouragement of moral surveillance. Human beings frequently harbor suspicions, misinterpret ambiguous sights, or stumble upon the private shortcomings of others. This legislation establishes that discovering another person's moral failure does not grant license to initiate a public scandal or mobilize legal sanctions.

Unless an individual possesses four independent eyewitnesses willing to testify to an overt public outrage, divine law demands concealment (*satr*) and private ethical counsel. Circulating unproven suspicions, even if the accuser privately believes them to be true, incurs the full wrath of the law. By making the cost of unsubstantiated speech catastrophic for the speaker, the Qur'an protects the domestic sanctuary, forcing society to prioritize peace and personal privacy over intrusive moral vigilantism. The dignity of the individual takes precedence over communal voyeurism.

---
## Sūrah an-Nūr 24:5

> except those who repent afterwards and mend their ways, then surely Allah is AllForgiving, Most Merciful.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Opening of Divine Grace to the Defamer**

Immediately following the three severe penalties decreed against false accusers, the divine voice introduces an avenue of redemption: *illā alladhīna tābū min baʿdi dhālika wa-aṣlaḥū*. The juxtaposition of rigorous judicial sanctions with immediate offers of divine mercy reflects the balanced architecture of Qur'anic justice. Law does not exist merely to crush the transgressor, but to restrain harm and rehabilitate the contrite heart.

Repentance (*tawbah*) is not treated as a trivial emotional confession muttered in private seclusion. The text explicitly conjoins repentance with *iṣlāḥ*—active rectification and moral amendment. The wrongdoer who wounded another person's reputation must publicly undo the damage they inflicted, acknowledging their falsehood and striving to restore the social peace their reckless speech shattered. Without tangible restitution and public vindication of the innocent, verbal expressions of sorrow remain spiritually hollow before God and humanity. The path of redemption requires concrete moral repair and an unambiguous public reversal of the lie.

**The Great Legal Debate Over the Scope of the Exception**

The placement of the exception clause *illā* (except) sparked one of the most famous debates in the history of Islamic legal theory. Verse 4 concluded with three distinct punitive measures: eighty lashes, the permanent rejection of legal testimony (*abadā*), and the characterization of the accusers as rebellious wrongdoers (*al-fāsiqūn*). Jurists debated whether the exception modifies all three clauses, only the last two, or solely the final sentence.

All scholars agreed that repentance cannot suspend the physical punishment of eighty lashes once the victim demands justice and the case reaches the magistrate. Physical penalties serve as deterrent rights of society that cannot be dissolved merely by private remorse. The dispute concentrated entirely on whether sincere repentance could restore the slanderer's civil right to give legal testimony in a court of law, establishing whether judicial disqualification is an irreversible sanction or a curable condition under divine law. This debate highlights the delicate tension between retributive deterrence and restorative justice across classical legal schools.

**The Majority Opinion on Civic Rehabilitation**

The majority of classical jurists—including Imām Mālik, Imām al-Shāfiʿī, and Imām Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal—held that repentance rehabilitates the offender both spiritually and civically. In their grammatical and legal analysis, the exception attaches naturally to the preceding clauses as a coherent whole. When a person repents, admits their slander to be false, and demonstrates upright character, they cease to be a *fāsiq* in the eyes of God, and their civil capacity to bear witness is fully restored.

They argued that divine mercy is comprehensive; if God accepts a person's return from the monstrous sin of idolatry or murder and permits them to testify, it is unreasonable to assume that divine law permanently disenfranchises a repentant slanderer. Permanent civil alienation would only foster bitterness and drive the offender into enduring antagonism against the community, whereas reintegration encourages genuine moral transformation and strengthens social cohesion. The community gains far more by restoring a reformed soul than by maintaining a permanent underclass of legal pariahs.

**The Ḥanafī Position on Permanent Disqualification**

In sharp contrast, Imām Abū Ḥanīfah and his school maintained that the exception applies exclusively to the final descriptive clause: *wa-ulāʾika humu al-fāsiqūn*. Under Ḥanafī jurisprudence, the slanderer who repents is forgiven by God and freed from the spiritual stain of rebellion in the Hereafter, but their legal testimony remains permanently inadmissible before earthly judges.

The Ḥanafīs based their ruling upon the word *abadā* (never/forever), which they regarded as an absolute, unqualified temporal barrier that grammatical exceptions cannot pierce. Furthermore, they pointed out the judicial danger of allowing slanderers back into the witness box: if a person once proved capable of inventing a malicious lie against a neighbor's chastity, courtroom prudence demands that their testimony never again determine the life, lineage, or property of another citizen. Civic caution must persist even when spiritual redemption is granted, ensuring that courtrooms maintain absolute credibility and protect future litigants from perjury.

**The Concrete Demonstration of Moral Rectification**

Classical authorities outlined the precise practical steps required to fulfill the condition of *aṣlaḥū* (mending their ways). Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī relate that the early jurists required the slanderer to present themselves publicly before the judicial assembly and openly declare: I lied in what I attributed to such a person, my accusation was false, and I have repented to God. Merely refraining from further slander is insufficient; the defamer must take positive ownership of their error and actively vindicate the victim's honor before the community.

Additionally, many jurists insisted upon a period of moral probation—frequently stipulated as one full year—during which the community could observe the individual's consistent adherence to truthfulness, prayer, and virtuous conduct. This probationary interval ensured that the repentance was sincere and not a calculated maneuver to regain social influence, safeguarding the courtroom from deceptive opportunism and confirming genuine spiritual renewal. Upright behavior over time remains the only reliable proof of internal transformation and true integrity.

**The Theological Harmony of Forgiveness and Wisdom**

The verse seals the legislative exception by invoking two profound divine names: *fa-inna Allāha ghafūrun raḥīm*, for surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. This divine signature provides the ultimate theological justification for the law's flexibility. God does not demand perfection from mortal servants; He demands accountability and the willingness to correct one's course after stumbling into sin.

The attribute of forgiveness (*ghafūr*) indicates that God conceals the past sins of the penitent, shielding them from ultimate ruin, while mercy (*raḥīm*) ensures that continuous divine assistance accompanies their moral rehabilitation. By ending the passage with these attributes, the text instructs the human community to reflect divine generosity in their social structures, welcoming back the contrite wrongdoer who has genuinely laboured to heal the wounds they inflicted upon their fellows. Grace remains the final word in divine governance, offering hope where human judgment often falters.

---
## Sūrah an-Nūr 24:6

> And those who accuse their wives ˹of adultery˺ but have no witness except themselves, the accuser must testify, swearing four times by Allah that he is telling the truth,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Intimate Crisis of Marital Allegation**

The revelation addresses a unique domestic predicament that ruptures the ordinary rules of evidence: *wa-alladhīna yarmūna azwājahum wa-lam yakun lahum shuhadāʾu illā anfusuhum*. The law of slander in 24:4 established an unyielding barrier against defamation, requiring four eyewitnesses and sentencing unproven accusers to eighty lashes. In typical public interactions, that standard shields innocent citizens from malice. Marriage, however, introduces an intimate sphere of life shielded from the public gaze. An act of marital infidelity occurs in private spaces where four independent observers are virtually never present.

A husband who discovers his wife in betrayal faces an agonizing dilemma. If he remains silent, he endures psychological devastation, risks raising a child not his own, and tolerates a desecrated marriage bed. If he speaks publicly without four external witnesses, the general statute of 24:4 would command that he be flogged with eighty lashes as a slanderer. This verse resolves the crisis by introducing the procedure of *liʿān* (mutual imprecation), providing a distinct judicial pathway tailored strictly to the spousal relationship. The law balances personal grief with institutional equity, acknowledging domestic vulnerability while preventing unchecked slander.

**The Substitution of Four Oaths for Four Eyewitnesses**

The legal mechanism substitutes four solemn oaths sworn by God in place of four external witnesses: *fa-shahādatu aḥadihim arbaʿu shahādātin bi-Allāh*. Each individual oath stands in the place of one eyewitness. By commanding the husband to swear four distinct times by the divine name, the law elevates personal testimony into a sacred legal deposition. Swearing by God four times establishes procedural equivalence with the four-witness standard required in ordinary allegations.

Classical jurists emphasized the ceremonial precision of this act. The husband must pronounce each oath distinctly in the presence of the presiding judge. He cannot compress the four declarations into a single consolidated statement, because each repetition represents an independent testimonial step. Divine law harnesses the profound spiritual reverence the believer holds for the Almighty, using solemn oaths to substitute for the sensory corroboration of external spectators. The four oaths constitute a complete judicial structure of proof, binding the speaker's mortal conscience to divine oversight.

**The Assertion of Truthfulness: innahū lamina al-ṣādiqīn**

The husband must conclude each of the four oaths with a definitive formula: *innahū lamina al-ṣādiqīn*, affirming that he is truly among the truthful in his accusation. Truthfulness (*ṣidq*) in the Qur'anic worldview is an objective reality linked to divine order. The husband does not merely state a subjective opinion or convey suspicion; he binds his identity directly to truthfulness under the gaze of the All-Hearing, All-Seeing Creator.

Perjury committed under such conditions is classified in classical jurisprudence as *al-yamīn al-ghamūs*—the catastrophic oath that submerges the swearer into divine condemnation. A believer who fears God recognizes that lying while repeatedly invoking the divine name invites immediate spiritual ruin. The formula leaves no room for ambiguity or hesitation; the accuser must profess absolute certainty regarding the veracity of the charge, acknowledging that God knows every hidden motive. False swearing under the divine name destroys the soul from within.

**The Jurisprudential Distinction Between Spouses and Strangers**

Islamic jurisprudence strictly confines the procedure of *liʿān* to legally married couples. Scholars across the major schools—including Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Abū Ḥanīfah—emphasized that a stranger who accuses a married woman without four eyewitnesses receives no exemption and is immediately sentenced to eighty lashes for *qadhf*. The exception is granted exclusively to a husband because of the existential interests at stake.

A husband bears legal and financial responsibility for the household, the maintenance of the wife, and the legitimacy of the offspring. If a child is born of an adulterous encounter, attributing that child to the husband impacts inheritance rights, familial prohibitions, and lineage integrity (*nasab*). A stranger shares none of these existential burdens. The law therefore grants the husband a unique procedural mechanism to protect his lineage without suffering the penalty of defamation, shielding family integrity from irreparable disruption while denying malicious third parties any opportunity to defame families.

**The Historical Context: The Dilemma of Hilāl ibn Umayyah**

The historical occasion for this legislation is preserved in authoritative traditions, including the report in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 4747 concerning Hilāl ibn Umayyah. Hilāl came before the Prophet ﷺ after witnessing his wife in compromise with another man. Upon hearing the claim, the Prophet ﷺ held firmly to the established law of slander, declaring: Either bring your proof or face the whip upon your back.

Hilāl cried out in distress, pleading that when a man sees such an outrage in his home, searching for four witnesses is impossible, and he expressed conviction that God would send down revelation to relieve his back from the whip. The descent of verses 6 through 9 provided immediate divine relief for the community, demonstrating how revelation intervened to address concrete human dilemmas while maintaining the transcendent majesty of divine justice. Divine revelation arrived precisely to establish equity where human law was trapped, offering a merciful resolution to an intolerable domestic crisis.

**The Moral Gravity of the Public Assembly**

Classical jurists stipulated that the administration of these oaths must occur in a public and venerated setting. Imām al-Shāfiʿī and Imām Mālik recommended that the ceremony be conducted in the central congregational mosque immediately following the afternoon prayer, surrounded by a gathering of upright believers.

Exposing the marital grievance to the solemn scrutiny of the community and the sanctity of the mosque impresses upon the accuser the terrifying weight of his words. The public setting strips away flippant bravado. Standing before fellow worshippers in the house of God, the husband is compelled to confront the eternal consequence of uttering a false oath before the Almighty, ensuring that litigation is approached with awe rather than reckless passion. The presence of the congregation guarantees that justice is solemn, transparent, and revered.

---
## Sūrah an-Nūr 24:7

> and a fifth oath that Allah may condemn him if he is lying.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Climactic Fifth Oath: The Seal of Conscience**

The procedural sequence of marital accusation reaches its decisive threshold in the fifth declaration: *wa-al-khāmisatu anna laʿnata Allāhi ʿalayhi in kāna mina al-kādhibīn*. The first four oaths fulfilled the evidentiary requirement, standing in place of four external witnesses. The fifth oath, however, introduces an entirely different dimension: self-imprecation. The husband explicitly calls down the divine curse upon his own soul if he has fabricated the accusation.

This structural separation between four declarative oaths and a fifth imprecatory oath transforms the proceeding from an ordinary legal dispute into an existential trial before the divine throne. In the four initial oaths, the man asserts his truthfulness; in the final oath, he voluntarily surrenders himself to divine retribution if he is lying. The fifth oath acts as the ultimate seal of conscience, demanding that the accuser stake his eternal destiny upon the truth of his charge, closing any psychological retreat. The soul of the accuser is placed directly on the scales of divine judgment.

**The Nature and Terror of Divine Condemnation (laʿnah)**

In the Qur'anic lexicon, the term *laʿnah* signifies far more than temporary anger or verbal rebuke; it denotes absolute expulsion and banishment from divine mercy. To be cursed by God is to be stripped of spiritual protection, abandoned in this worldly life, and consigned to everlasting torment in the Hereafter. A believer regards divine mercy as the singular hope for salvation.

To deliberately invoke the curse of God upon oneself is the most terrifying utterance a human being can formulate. Classical scholars observed that an individual of sound faith would far prefer enduring eighty lashes, public disgrace, or worldly imprisonment than uttering a phrase that invites total exile from God's grace. By installing *laʿnah* as the culmination of the husband's testimony, the divine law erects an impenetrable spiritual barrier against calculated deceit and frivolous litigation, elevating the sanctity of the courtroom to an encounter with cosmic consequences. No temporal gain can compensate for the forfeiture of divine grace.

**The Judicial Admonition Before the Fifth Oath**

The profound gravity of this final oath is reflected in the procedural safeguards instituted by the Prophet ﷺ. In authentic traditions, such as the narration in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 4748, the Prophet ﷺ instructed the judicial authority to intervene directly before the fifth declaration. The judge places a hand over the man's mouth or issues a stern verbal admonition: Pause, for this fifth oath brings the inescapable consequence of God's wrath, and the punishment of the Hereafter is far more severe than the shame of this world.

This required pause demonstrates the humane sensitivity embedded within Islamic jurisprudence. Even after an accuser has completed four formal oaths, the law offers a final moment of reflection, encouraging him to withdraw the charge if motivated by fleeting anger or unfounded suspicion. The legal system seeks truth, not the spiritual destruction of its citizens, providing every opportunity for conscience to halt irreversible calamity before the final threshold is crossed.

**The Legal Consequences of Completing the Husband's Oaths**

The formal utterance of the fifth oath triggers immediate legal consequences. First, the husband is officially shielded from the statutory punishment of eighty lashes decreed in 24:4 for unproven slander. His sworn imprecations legally satisfy his obligation of proof as an accusing spouse. Second, if the husband accompanied his accusation with a formal renunciation of paternity (*nafy al-walad*), the child is legally detached from his lineage.

The child is henceforth attributed exclusively to the mother, inheriting from her and her relatives, while the husband is freed from child maintenance obligations. However, the completion of the husband's five oaths does not automatically establish the wife's guilt in a manner that warrants her execution or flogging. Rather, it shifts the evidentiary burden entirely onto her shoulders, requiring her to respond through the procedure outlined in the succeeding verse, maintaining a dynamic procedural equilibrium between the rights of the spouses.

**The Irrevocable Dissolution of the Domestic Bond**

The completion of the husband's imprecation results in the immediate, permanent termination of the marriage. Classical jurists unanimously agreed that a marital union cannot endure once a husband has formally invoked the curse of God upon himself regarding his wife's chastity. The mutual trust and affection required to sustain a righteous home are irrevocably shattered.

Imām Mālik, Imām al-Shāfiʿī, and Imām Aḥmad maintained that the separation resulting from *liʿān* creates an eternal prohibition (*taḥrīm muʾabbad*). The couple can never remarry under any circumstances, even if both later retract their oaths or express mutual forgiveness. Abū Ḥanīfah held that the dissolution requires a formal decree from the judge, but concurred that the marital bond is irreparably severed. The permanence of the separation serves as a sobering reminder of the finality of marital slander, permanently guarding the home against toxic reconciliation and ongoing recrimination.

**Self-Imprecation as the Boundary of Human Adjudication**

This legislation illustrates the philosophical boundary of human judicial authority. Human courts operate strictly upon outward evidence, documentation, and credible testimony. When a dispute occurs in total privacy, beyond the reach of external witnesses, earthly adjudication reaches its limit. Rather than resorting to coercive torture, forced confessions, or invasive surveillance, Islamic law entrusts the final verdict to the Lord of the unseen.

By placing the outcome under the divine curse, the legal system acknowledges that while human judges may be deceived by convincing rhetoric, God cannot be misled. The accuser walks away from the courtroom knowing that if he spoke falsely, he carries an active divine curse upon his soul, ensuring that divine justice will inevitably overtake him in this life or the next. The judicial process preserves human dignity by refusing to intrude where God alone judges, leaving the unseen to the All-Knowing Creator.

---
## Sūrah an-Nūr 24:8

> For her to be spared the punishment, she must swear four times by Allah that he is telling a lie,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Parity of Defense: Averting the Statutory Punishment**

The revelation establishes an immediate procedural defense for the accused woman: *wa-yadraʾu ʿanhā al-ʿadhāba an tashhada arbaʿa shahādātin bi-Allāh*. The verb *yadraʾu* signifies repelling, warding off, or deflecting an impending blow. Once the husband completes his five solemn oaths, a grave legal presumption arises against the wife. In many ancient and contemporary legal traditions, a husband's formal accusation was sufficient to condemn a woman without granting her equal recourse.

Divine law rejects this unilateral imbalance. The wife is endowed with autonomous legal capacity to neutralize the husband's accusation entirely through her own testimony. By swearing four times by the name of God, she deflects the punishment that would otherwise follow. This structure guarantees that no woman can be convicted, disenfranchised, or penalized based solely upon the sworn assertions of her husband, establishing absolute procedural fairness and moral equity within the courtroom, protecting the vulnerable against unilateral power and arbitrary condemnation. The statute affirms her independent legal personhood before the court of law.

**The Symmetry of Four Oaths of Denial**

The legal architecture constructed here exhibits precise numerical and evidentiary symmetry. The husband swore four times that he was speaking the truth; the wife now swears four times that he is a liar: *innahū lamina al-kādhibīn*. Four positive assertions of misconduct are met and cancelled by four negative assertions of innocence.

In classical Islamic evidentiary jurisprudence, the wife's sworn word carries legal power identical to that of the husband. Her testimony is not discounted on the basis of gender or social standing. Each of her four oaths systematically neutralizes one of his four oaths. This remarkable balance emphasizes that when private marital disputes enter the public courtroom, both spouses stand as equal moral agents before the divine law, each possessing the exact same power to make sworn representations before the judicial assembly, demonstrating the impartial nature of divine justice across all strata of society.

**The Definition of al-ʿadhāb in the Context of Liʿān**

Classical jurists engaged in thorough analysis regarding the precise nature of the punishment (*al-ʿadhāb*) that the wife's oaths avert. The majority of scholars, including Imām al-Shāfiʿī, Imām Mālik, and Imām Aḥmad, held that *al-ʿadhāb* refers to the statutory corporal punishment for adultery established in 24:2. If the husband has completed his oaths and the wife refuses to swear her counter-oaths, she is deemed to have implicitly conceded the accusation, thereby facing the prescribed penalty.

Conversely, Imām Abū Ḥanīfah and his disciples argued that she is not subjected to corporal punishment upon refusal, but rather detained by the judicial authorities until she either takes the oaths of *liʿān* or admits the truth of the charge. This disagreement underscores the extreme reluctance of classical jurists to implement corporal penalties in the presence of evidentiary doubt, demonstrating how both views sought to honor the sanctity of human life and bodily integrity, avoiding punitive excess whenever ambiguity remains in the judicial record of the case.

**The Burden of Public Counter-Testimony**

Taking these four oaths imposes a profound psychological and emotional burden upon the accused woman. Standing before the assembled community in the central mosque, under the watchful gaze of the judge and fellow believers, she must voice her denial with unwavering conviction. This public visibility ensures that her vindication is as open, decisive, and recorded as her husband's accusation.

The law does not permit her exoneration to occur behind closed doors or through private compromises that might leave her reputation tainted by lingering whispers. By taking the witness stand publicly and invoking the supreme name of God four times, she asserts her honor in the most transparent forum available, compelling the entire community to recognize that the charge has been legally and spiritually repelled. Her voice resounds with full legal authority across the sacred precinct, clearing her name in the eyes of all observers and recording her complete innocence.

**The Rejection of Coerced Submission and Domestic Tyranny**

This statutory defense serves as a powerful bulwark against spousal abuse and domestic extortion. In patriarchal societies, unscrupulous men might employ false accusations to coerce a wife into forfeiting her dowry (*mahr*), relinquishing custody of her children, or submitting to intolerable marital conditions. Without the procedural protection of verse 8, a husband could weaponize the legal system to ruin his wife without fear of consequence.

By granting the woman the unilateral power to ward off punishment through her own sworn testimony, the Qur'an dismantles the husband's ability to act as judge and jury over her fate. Even if the husband is wealthy, influential, and socially prominent, his status cannot override her sworn denial. Divine law ensures that an innocent woman retains full sovereignty over her legal defense, protected from domestic intimidation and institutional coercion, affirming her god-given dignity within the marital framework.

**The Neutralization of Earthly Penalties**

When the wife completes her four oaths, an absolute evidentiary equilibrium is achieved. Human judges possess no divine insight to determine with absolute certainty which spouse spoke the truth and which spouse committed perjury. The competing sets of oaths effectively cancel each other out in the realm of tangible evidence.

In accordance with the foundational legal principle that penalties must be averted whenever doubt exists, the court refrains from punishing either party. The husband cannot be lashed for *qadhf* because he swore his five oaths, and the wife cannot be punished for adultery because she swore her four counter-oaths. Earthly justice gracefully bows before the limits of human knowledge, leaving the final moral reckoning to the eternal judgment of God, preserving the peace of the community without spilling blood upon unproven claims, resting securely in divine omniscience.

---
## Sūrah an-Nūr 24:9

> and a fifth oath that Allah may be displeased with her if he is telling the truth.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Culminating Imprecation of the Wife**

The procedural sequence of marital defense reaches its terrifying climax in the fifth declaration: *wa-al-khāmisata anna ghaḍaba Allāhi ʿalayhā in kāna mina al-ṣādiqīn*. In the preceding verse, the woman pronounced four distinct oaths swearing that her husband was lying, thereby establishing an evidentiary counterweight to his charges. In this fifth step, she shifts from factual denial to direct self-imprecation. She explicitly calls down the divine wrath upon her own soul if her husband has spoken the truth.

This symmetrical culmination completes the judicial architecture of *liʿān*. Just as the husband was required to seal his testimony by invoking the divine curse, the wife must seal her defense by invoking divine wrath. Classical jurists noted that this fifth declaration transforms her testimony into an existential wager. She does not merely assert innocence before a human magistrate; she surrenders herself entirely to the righteous anger of the Almighty if she is guilty of the betrayal, leaving her eternal fate in divine hands.

**The Theological Distinction Between laʿnah and ghaḍab**

Classical exegetes, including al-Zamakhsharī and Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī, observed a profound linguistic and theological distinction in the divine choice of words. In verse 7, the husband invokes *laʿnah* (the curse of God), whereas in verse 9, the wife invokes *ghaḍab* (the wrath of God). While both terms denote severe divine displeasure, *ghaḍab* represents an even more intense and immediate manifestation of divine retribution than *laʿnah*.

A curse signifies expulsion and banishment from divine mercy, whereas wrath denotes active, consuming divine anger. Commentators explained that if a woman commits adultery and subsequently stands in the house of God to swear false oaths, her offense compounds marital betrayal with sacrilegious perjury. By invoking *ghaḍab*, the revelation emphasizes the catastrophic spiritual peril awaiting any woman who would dare use the divine name to conceal private corruption and deceive the community, facing the raw fury of heaven.

**The Prophetic Interruption and the Moment of Trembling**

The Sunnah preserves a moving record of the psychological gravity that attended this fifth oath. In authoritative traditions, including the report in Sunan Abī Dāwūd 2256 and related accounts in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, the Prophet ﷺ commanded the presiding authority to pause the woman before she uttered the final formula. The judge warned her: Fear God, for this fifth oath brings the inescapable consequence of divine retribution, and the punishment of the Hereafter is far more severe than worldly disgrace.

In one historical instance, when warned of the finality of this fifth oath, the woman hesitated and drew back, causing the spectators to believe she was about to confess. However, she resolved herself and proclaimed: I will not disgrace my family for the rest of time, and proceeded to complete the oath. This prophetic intervention illustrates the deep pastoral concern embedded in Islamic jurisprudence, which actively provides litigants every opportunity to step back from eternal ruin, prioritizing genuine salvation over hasty procedural closure.

**The Complete Termination of the Spousal Relationship**

The formal completion of the wife's fifth oath repels the statutory penalty of adultery from her person, but it marks the immediate and irreversible termination of the marriage. The husband and wife are instantly separated by operation of law. Classical jurists unanimously agreed that a household cannot endure when spouses have traded public imprecations of divine curses and divine wrath.

Regarding financial rights, the Prophet ﷺ ruled that the woman retains her full deferred dowry (*mahr*) if the marriage was consummated. When the accusing husband inquired about the recovery of his wealth, the Prophet ﷺ declared: The dowry is hers in exchange for what you enjoyed of her intimacy; if you spoke the truth, you have no claim to it, and if you lied, it is even further from your reach. The law scrupulously protects the woman's financial entitlements even amidst domestic devastation, preventing the husband from using defamation as a financial weapon.

**The Lineage and Custody of the Child**

When the process of *liʿān* involves a husband disowning a child born of the marriage, the completion of the oaths establishes definitive legal rulings regarding lineage and custody. The Prophet ﷺ decreed that the child is attributed exclusively to the mother: *al-walad li-ummih*. The child inherits from the mother and her heirs, and the mother inherits from the child, while all legal ties to the former husband are severed.

Crucially, the law erects a formidable shield around the mother and child against future slander. Anyone who dares cast aspersions on the child's legitimacy or defames the mother based upon the severed marriage is subjected to the full penalty of eighty lashes for *qadhf*. Divine law ensures that the child is not rendered an outcast or subjected to social stigmatization, preserving their dignity through legitimate maternal kinship, guaranteeing that no innocent newborn bears the stigma of parental conflict.

**The Divine Horizon Beyond Human Courts**

The conclusion of the *liʿān* procedure reveals the philosophical boundary where human jurisprudence surrenders to divine sovereignty. Both husband and wife have made their solemn representations under oath; both have staked their souls upon their veracity before the assembled community. Earthly judges possess no metaphysical instrument to verify what transpired behind closed doors.

The court declares the proceedings closed, imposing neither lashes nor stoning, and dissolves the marriage. The two litigants depart into society, each knowing that while earthly institutions have finished their work, divine justice remains perpetually active. The liar carries an inescapable spiritual burden, while the truthful party rests secure in the knowledge that God is the ultimate witness who never allows truth to be eclipsed. In the divine presence, no secret remains hidden and no deceit escapes ultimate reckoning.

---
## Sūrah an-Nūr 24:10

> ˹You would have suffered,˺ had it not been for Allah’s grace and mercy upon you, and had Allah not been Accepting of Repentance, All-Wise.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Dramatic Ellipsis of Divine Grace**

The verse pauses the detailed legislative discourse with a profound rhetorical exclamation: *wa-lawlā faḍlu Allāhi ʿalaykum wa-raḥmatuhū wa-anna Allāha tawwābun ḥakīm*. Grammatically, the conditional particle *lawlā* (were it not for) is left without an explicit result clause (*ḥadhf al-jawāb*). In classical Arabic rhetoric (*balāghah*), omitting the consequence is an intentional literary device designed to convey an outcome too immense and terrifying for words to encompass.

The omission forces the mind to imagine what would have occurred had divine grace not intervened. Without divine guidance and restraint, the community would have descended into violent chaos, husbands and wives would have destroyed one another through uncontrolled vengeance, false swearers would have been struck dead by immediate heavenly wrath, and social trust would have disintegrated completely. The rhetorical silence underscores the vast gulf between divine mercy and human ruin, reminding humanity that every moment of social peace is an unearned gift from the Almighty. Without divine forbearance, no human community could long survive its own moral failures, rash accusations, and internal strife.

**Grace (faḍl) and Mercy (raḥmah) as Social Bulwarks**

The pairing of divine grace (*faḍl*) and divine mercy (*raḥmah*) identifies the theological foundation upon which all communal stability rests. Grace represents unearned divine generosity, while mercy represents active protection against destruction and suffering. In the immediate context of marital disputes and false accusations, God's grace manifested in the revelation of clear, humane legal procedures.

Before this legislation descended, people were trapped between the rigid demand for four external witnesses and the agony of silent domestic betrayal. By revealing the precise statutes of *qadhf* and *liʿān*, God provided an equitable judicial exit that preserves human honor while containing private grief. Divine law is not a punitive burden imposed upon humanity; it is a manifestation of pure divine benevolence that rescues human civilization from its own destructive impulses, providing order where human passions would otherwise reign supreme and produce endless cycles of bloodshed and vendetta across generations.

**The Attribute of Tawwāb: Welcoming the Errant Soul**

The verse highlights the divine name *al-Tawwāb*—the One who constantly turns toward His servants with forgiveness and acceptance of repentance. Human beings are fragile, emotional creatures who routinely succumb to anger, jealousy, suspicion, and moral weakness. In the heat of domestic discord or communal panic, individuals often utter words they later regret or make accusations born of hasty judgment.

By invoking *al-Tawwāb*, the revelation reminds the community that legal conflict does not represent the end of spiritual hope. Even when a marriage ends in the acrimony of mutual imprecation, or when a person stumbles into false speech, the door of repentance remains wide open before the Creator. God welcomes the contrite servant who returns with genuine remorse, wiping away past transgressions and offering a pathway toward spiritual renewal and reconciliation with the divine reality. Sincere repentance can heal the deepest wounds of the soul, transforming personal ruin into spiritual elevation and enduring peace with God.

**Divine Wisdom (ḥakīm) in Legal Delineation**

The verse concludes with the attribute *al-Ḥakīm*—the All-Wise. Every statute, procedural condition, and evidentiary standard established in the preceding verses reflects flawless divine wisdom. Human legislation frequently oscillates between destructive extremes: either indulging in permissive laxity that trivializes marital sanctity, or enforcing draconian measures that violate human dignity and destroy privacy.

Divine wisdom alone strikes the perfect equilibrium. It prescribes severe corporal deterrence for public licentiousness while demanding an almost insurmountable four-witness threshold; it establishes eighty lashes for unproven slander while creating a specialized oath-based exit for spouses. No human assembly could have calibrated legal mechanisms that simultaneously defend chastity, safeguard domestic sanctuary, protect lineage, and shield reputations from gossip with such sublime balance. Divine legislation remains peerless in its harmony and justice, answering human needs across every generation with absolute equity.

**A Transition Between Private Slander and Public Conspiracy**

Structurally, verse 10 functions as a monumental pivot within the architecture of Sūrah an-Nūr. It brings the intimate legislation concerning marital accusation and *liʿān* (vv. 6–9) to a reverent close, while simultaneously preparing the audience for the national crisis of the Grand Slander (*al-Ifk*) detailed in verses 11 through 26.

The reminder of divine grace applies in both directions. It looks backward to the salvation of fragile households from murderous jealousy, and it looks forward to the miraculous preservation of the Prophet's ﷺ household and the young Muslim state from a coordinated campaign of political and moral sabotage. The community is reminded that its survival through every trial—whether domestic or civic—is owed entirely to transcendent divine intervention. The mercy that saves the individual family is the same mercy that guards the entire civilization against subversive collapse and communal hysteria.

**The Restraint of Immediate Retribution**

A deeper theological lesson lies in the divine restraint that characterizes God's governance. Had God acted with swift, uncompromising retribution, every individual who entertained a wicked suspicion or uttered a hasty word would have been consumed by instant punishment. Instead, the All-Wise Creator grants respite, providing time for truth to emerge, for consciences to awaken, and for repentance to be enacted.

This divine forbearance provides an enduring model for human judicial authorities and communal leaders. Justice must never be executed in a spirit of impulsive wrath or hasty vengeance. By exercising measured restraint, upholding procedural safeguards, and allowing space for moral reflection, human institutions reflect the gracious and wise governance of the Almighty, ensuring that justice heals rather than destroys the social fabric. Patience is the hallmark of divine wisdom, sustaining the mortal world against sudden destruction and nurturing the seeds of moral renewal.

---
## Sūrah an-Nūr 24:11

> Indeed, those who came up with that ˹outrageous˺ slander are a group of you. Do not think this is bad for you. Rather, it is good for you. They will be punished, each according to their share of the sin. As for their mastermind, he will suffer a tremendous punishment.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Anatomy of the Outrageous Slander (al-Ifk)**

The revelation confronts the most severe crisis of reputation in early Islamic history: *inna alladhīna jāʾū bi-al-ifki ʿuṣbatun minkum*. In the Arabic language, *al-ifk* denotes the most heinous, inverted falsehood—a lie that completely turns reality upside down. The crisis occurred following the military expedition of Banū al-Muṣṭaliq. The Prophet's wife ʿĀʾishah was inadvertently left behind in the desert when her enclosed litter was mounted onto her camel without attendants noticing her absence, as she had stepped aside to search for a lost necklace.

When the pious and valorous companion Ṣafwān ibn al-Muʿaṭṭal, tasked with following the army to collect fallen belongings, discovered the Mother of the Believers asleep in the desert, he lowered his camel, turned his face in modest reverence, and led her back to Madinah in broad daylight. Enemies of the faith seized upon this innocent occurrence, fabricating a monstrous accusation of infidelity that circulated through the city for an entire month, wounding the Prophet ﷺ and throwing the community into profound turmoil.

**The Betrayal from Within: A Faction Among You**

The verse identifies the perpetrators with an unsettling phrase: *ʿuṣbatun minkum*—a cohesive group or faction among you. The assault did not come from pagan Quraysh marching across the sands, nor from hostile empires on distant borders; it was engineered within the civic body of Madinah itself. While the chief instigators were hypocrites (*munāfiqūn*) seeking to assassinate the moral authority of the Prophet ﷺ, several sincere believers fell victim to the hysteria and participated in circulating the rumor.

The text emphasizes this internal origin to warn the community of the lethal danger of informational subversion. When citizens turn upon one another with malicious gossip, they inflict damage far more insidious than physical warfare. Weaponized rumors erode mutual trust, dismantle social solidarity, and undermine legitimate leadership from within. The inclusion of the phrase *minkum* served as a painful rebuke, forcing the community to confront the moral rot that had been allowed to fester within its own borders.

**The Paradox of Divine Purpose: Evil Transformed into Good**

The verse introduces a breathtaking theological reorientation: *lā taḥsabūhu sharran lakum bal huwa khayrun lakum*, do not think this is bad for you; rather, it is good for you. To the human observer, the month of agonizing silence and malicious whispers appeared to be pure catastrophe. ʿĀʾishah fell grievously ill with grief, the Prophet ﷺ endured profound distress, and the household of Abū Bakr suffered immense social humiliation.

Yet divine wisdom reveals that apparent tragedy was transformed into comprehensive communal triumph. The crisis exposed the covert network of hypocrites, stripping away their facade; it tested and purified the faith of the sincere believers; it produced timeless constitutional legislation protecting personal honor and governing evidence; and it resulted in ten verses of eternal revelation that forever established ʿĀʾishah's purity in the recitation of the faithful until the Day of Resurrection. What malice intended for destruction, God turned into an enduring source of legal and spiritual guidance.

**Individual Culpability and the Measurement of Sin**

The text articulates a fundamental principle of divine justice: *li-kulli imriʾin minhum mā iktasaba mina al-ithm*. Sin is not an indiscriminate collective cloud in the sight of God; it is measured with absolute precision according to individual agency, intent, and action. Those who originated the fabrication, those who amplified it through casual dinner conversation, and those who listened with passive amusement each bore a distinct degree of moral culpability (*mā iktasaba*).

This precise apportionment warns believers that participation in communal sin cannot be excused by claiming one was merely following the crowd. Each soul stands individually accountable for the words it uttered, the ears it lent, and the intentions it harbored. While social dynamics often encourage individuals to dissolve their conscience into mob behavior, divine judgment isolates each actor, demanding an accounting for every particle of harm inflicted upon another human being's dignity.

**The Mastermind and the Tremendous Punishment**

The verse singles out the chief architect of the slander for unique condemnation: *wa-alladhī tawallā kibrahū minhum lahū ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm*. Commentators across the classical tradition, including al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Qurṭubī, unanimously identify this figure as ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy ibn Salūl, the chief of the hypocrites in Madinah. Unlike sincere companions such as Ḥassān ibn Thābit, Misṭaḥ ibn Uthāthah, and Ḥamnah bint Jaḥsh—who stumbled into the rumor out of human weakness and were subsequently purified through repentance and the statutory eighty lashes—Ibn Ubayy acted with calculated political malice.

Ibn Ubayy orchestrated the campaign to fracture the Muslim community, discredit the prophetic household, and destabilize the state. Because his crime was rooted in cynical hypocrisy and unrepentant enmity against God and His Messenger, the verse promises him a *ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm*—a tremendous, overwhelming punishment in the lowest depths of the Hellfire, where no intercession or mitigation will reach him.

**The Divine Vindication of Innocence**

The resolution of the crisis demonstrates the ultimate sovereignty of divine advocacy. In her famous narration in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 4750, ʿĀʾishah remarked with profound humility that she hoped the Prophet ﷺ would see a dream in which God would clear her, but she considered herself far too insignificant for God to send down verses of the Qur'an that would be recited in prayers forever.

Divine justice, however, does not measure human worth by worldly calculations. God intervened directly from above the heavens to shatter the slander, vindicating an innocent woman and cementing her status as a paragon of purity. This direct divine intervention teaches every oppressed and slandered soul across history that when human advocates are silent and earthly institutions fail, the Creator of the universe remains the ultimate defender of the righteous, guaranteeing that truth will ultimately prevail over falsehood.

---
## Sūrah an-Nūr 24:12

> If only the believing men and women had thought well of one another, when you heard this ˹rumour˺, and said, “This is clearly ˹an outrageous˺ slander!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rebuke of Passive Credulity: lawlā**

The divine address shifts from historical narrative to direct, piercing admonition: *lawlā idh samiʿtumūhu ẓanna al-muʾminūna wa-al-muʾminātu bi-anfusihim khayrā*. In classical Arabic grammar, the particle *lawlā* followed by a past-tense verb functions as an instrument of profound reproach (*tawbīkh*), demanding to know why a mandatory ethical duty was neglected. The Qur'an rebukes the community not merely for originating false speech, but for the moral passivity that permitted scandalous rumors to circulate unchecked.

Listening to slander with receptive indulgence or polite silence is treated by divine revelation as an active moral failure. A healthy society cannot survive if its citizens operate as passive conduits for character assassination. The text demands that believers exercise critical spiritual discernment the very moment a harmful report reaches their ears. When an allegation contradicts the established character of the accused, credulity ceases to be harmless naivety; it becomes complicity in evil, facilitating the spread of falsehood across the social fabric and eroding the communal peace.

**The Organic Unity of the Community: bi-anfusihim**

The verse employs an extraordinary phrase to describe the social bond of the faithful: *ẓanna al-muʾminūna wa-al-muʾminātu bi-anfusihim khayrā*—literally, that they should think well of their own selves. Classical exegetes, including Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī, emphasized that the Qur'an portrays the community of believers as an indivisible, living organism. When a believer considers a fellow believer, they are gazing upon an extension of their own spiritual selfhood.

This principle echoes the authentic prophetic teaching in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2586: The believers in their mutual love, mercy, and compassion are like a single body; when one limb aches, the entire body responds with sleeplessness and fever. To entertain malicious rumors about a brother or sister in faith is to harbor poison against one's own flesh. True solidarity demands that an attack on the honor of another believer be felt and resisted with the exact same protective instinct one would deploy to defend one's personal reputation, shielding one's brethren as one would shield oneself against disgrace.

**The Obligation of Ḥusn al-Ẓann (Positive Presumption)**

The verse codifies the foundational ethical duty of *ḥusn al-ẓann*—assuming the best of fellow believers. In Islamic legal and moral philosophy, innocence and integrity constitute the baseline reality of every Muslim (*al-aṣl barāʾat al-dhimmah*). Suspicion without definitive proof is strictly forbidden, as emphasized in 49:12. When an outrageous accusation is leveled against individuals whose lives are characterized by piety and moral excellence, the default assumption must always favor their purity.

To discard a proven lifetime of virtue in favor of an unverified rumor is an act of intellectual and spiritual corruption. Believers are commanded to anchor their judgments in established character rather than sensational whispers. The phrase *khayrā* (good) requires that when evidence is ambiguous or absent, the mind must immediately incline toward positive interpretations, shielding the honor of upright citizens from casual defamation and refusing to entertain vile speculation about their hidden lives.

**The Courage of Immediate Verbal Denunciation**

The revelation does not permit moral resistance to remain confined to internal thoughts; it commands outward verbal resistance: *wa-qālū hādhā ifkun mubīn*. The believers were required to actively articulate their rejection, declaring aloud: This is clearly an outrageous slander! Passive internal skepticism is insufficient to dismantle social hysteria. The voice of truth must be spoken boldly in the public square.

When gossip is met with open denunciation, its momentum is shattered. Slanderers rely upon polite silence and receptive smiles to spread their poison from ear to ear. When an upright believer interrupts the conversation to name the gossip for what it is—a manifest lie—the social atmosphere changes instantly.
